/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/experiments.tex:23:Sigmoid regression yields $\alpha_c = 0.483 \pm 0.006$ ($R^2 = 0.969$; Figure~\ref{fig:hero}b); cross-seed consistency (3/3 seeds: $0/8{\to}6/8$) and specification-invariance across 1{,}160 configurations (Appendix~\ref{app:spec_curve}) argue for a genuine transition (Fisher exact: $p = 3.45 \times 10^{-11}$).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/experiments.tex:59:The PermG dose-response profile (Figure~\ref{fig:hero}b; Appendix~\ref{app:dose_response}) reveals three confirmed regimes---\emph{blind} ($\alpha \leq 0.45$, 0/8), \emph{onset} ($\alpha \approx 0.50$, 6/8), and \emph{detection} ($\alpha{\geq}0.75$, 6/8)---plus a non-monotonic anomaly at $\alpha \approx 0.60$ (0/8).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/experiments.tex:82:Forward detection reaches ${\geq}6/8$ in 11/15 conditions (Figure~\ref{fig:hero}d).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/introduction.tex:21:\label{fig:hero}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/introduction.tex:24:We systematically map this landscape on Pythia-410M~\citep{biderman2023pythia} across 13 contamination levels with multi-seed replication (Figure~\ref{fig:hero}).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/introduction.tex:25:Our most consequential finding concerns method sensitivity: five statistical methods applied to identical data yield null-control FPR spanning 7\% to 94\% ($\kappa{=}0.185$; Figure~\ref{fig:hero}c), demonstrating that single-method analyses cannot reliably distinguish real signals from artifacts.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/introduction.tex:26:Permutation Granger causality (PermG), the lowest-FPR method (7.4\%, CI $[2.1\%, 17.9\%]$, 9~seeds), reveals structured terrain rather than smooth degradation: detection is absent through $\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps from 0/8 to 6/8 significant pairs within a 2-percentage-point interval (Figure~\ref{fig:hero}a), before a non-monotonic dip and eventual reliable detection above a power-law boundary $\alpha_c(T)$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:alpha-c-theory}).

7	If distributional collapse follows a predictable ordering across metrics, the earliest-responding metric can serve as a canary signal.
8	We formalize known metric properties---entropy as a continuous indicator of mode concentration, diversity as a threshold indicator of lexical coverage---under mode amplification~\citep{shumailov2024curse,dohmatob2024tale} (derivations in Appendix~\ref{app:cascade_theory}).
9	
10	\begin{designprinciple}[Entropy responds before diversity]\label{thm:entropy_leads}
11	Under mode amplification: \textup{(a)} entropy can decrease while the $\epsilon$-effective support $S_\epsilon(p) {=} |\{i: p_i > \epsilon\}|$ (hence distinct-$n$) is unchanged; \textup{(b)} if distinct-$n$ decreases, entropy has already decreased.
12	\end{designprinciple}
13	
14	The asymmetry arises because entropy responds to any mass redistribution (first-order), while diversity changes only when a token probability crosses the generation threshold~$\epsilon$; the margin $\gamma = \min_{i: p_i > \epsilon}(p_i - \epsilon)$ quantifies how much shift diversity absorbs silently.
15	
16	\begin{designprinciple}[Diversity lag $\geq 1$ generation]\label{prop:div_lag}
17	If the per-generation perturbation satisfies $\alpha\|\mathcal{T}(p_t) - p_t\|_\infty < \gamma(p_t, \epsilon)$ through entropy onset $t_H$, then diversity onset $t_D \geq t_H + 1$.
18	\end{designprinciple}
19	
20	ECE co-moves with entropy at zero lag ($\Delta\mathrm{ECE} \approx \Delta C > 0$ whenever $\Delta\bar{H} < 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:cascade_theory}).
21	The predicted cascade $\{H, \mathrm{ECE}\} \to \mathrm{Distinct}$-$n$ holds in 14/15 experiments at $\alpha \geq 0.30$: entropy and ECE onset at generation~1, distinct-$n$ at generation~2 ($t_H{=}t_{\mathrm{ECE}}{=}1$, $t_D{=}2$ uniformly; \S\ref{sec:regime}; Appendix~\ref{app:moment_cascade}).
22	The assumption requires monotonic entropy decline; SmolLM3-3B (Table~\ref{tab:generalization}) provides an empirical counter-example.
23	
24	\subsection{Design Considerations}
25	\label{sec:pitfalls}
26	
27	We use Granger causality~\citep{granger1969investigating} to test temporal precedence---whether past canary values improve prediction of downstream metrics---not mechanistic causation; bivariate Granger tests cannot exclude confounders~\citep{shojaie2022granger}.
28	Lead-lag analysis in iterative model collapse is fragile along three axes:
29	\emph{Method sensitivity}: five detection methods applied to identical data yield forward significance rates spanning 0\% to 92\%, with $\kappa{=}0.185$.
30	\emph{Spurious regression}: first-differencing eliminates canary$\to$perplexity associations ($F{\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$) while preserving canary$\to$diversity (24/24$\to$24/24)~\citep{granger1974spurious}.
31	\emph{Regime dependence}: the same Granger test shifts from 0/8 to 6/8 significant pairs within a 2-percentage-point interval.
32	
33	\subsection{Method Sensitivity Analysis}
34	\label{sec:mmct}
35	
36	We compare five lead-lag detection methods---naive cross-correlation (NaiveXCorr), differenced cross-correlation (DiffXCorr), threshold onset, permutation Granger causality (PermG), and Toda--Yamamoto~\citep{toda1995statistical}---in a multi-method comparison~\citep{simonsohn2020specification} (algorithm in Appendix~\ref{app:mmct}), calibrated against null-control ($\alpha{=}0.0$) data.
37	Pooled null-control FPR across nine seeds (54 pairs): PermG 7.4\%, DiffXCorr 14.8\%, Threshold 24.1\%, Toda--Yamamoto 33.3\%, NaiveXCorr 94.4\% (Appendix~\ref{app:fpr_independence}).
38	PermG (4/54; 95\% CI $[2.1\%, 17.9\%]$) achieves the lowest FPR, combining directional separation with consistent detection at $\alpha \geq 0.50$ (Shapley analysis: Appendix~\ref{app:shapley}).
39	Detection results use equal-weight majority voting ($K{\geq}3$ of 5 methods) with forward-direction filtering; $K{=}3$ yields 11.1\% FPR with 75\% true positive rate (TPR) at $\alpha{=}1.0$ (Table~\ref{tab:mmct_k_operating}).
40	
41	\paragraph{Permutation Granger causality.}
42	PermG tests whether past values of canary metric $X$ improve prediction of downstream metric $Y$ beyond $Y$'s own history~\citep{shojaie2022granger}, using 5{,}000 permutations~\citep{phipson2010permutation} on first-differenced series with Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) FDR correction at $q{=}0.05$ across 8 canary$\to$downstream pairs.
43	The permutation null controls Type~I error by construction; BH-FDR remains valid under positive regression dependence on a subset (PRDS)~\citep{benjamini2001control}.
44	Empirically, 4/54 null-control rejections across nine seeds confirm adequate size control (7/9 seeds yield 0~FP; Appendix~\ref{app:fpr_independence}).
45	
46	\subsection{Detection Boundary}
47	\label{sec:theory}
48	
49	The detection boundary is consistent with power-law scaling in observation length:
50	\begin{equation}
51	\alpha_c(T) = A \cdot T^{-\gamma},
52	\label{eq:alpha-c}
53	\end{equation}
54	Under an empirical sub-linear signal model $S(\alpha) = c \cdot \alpha^\beta$ ($\beta < 1$), the exponent satisfies $\gamma = 1/(2\beta)$:
55	\begin{equation}
56	\alpha_c(T) = A \cdot T^{-1/(2\beta)},
57	\label{eq:alpha-c-theory}
58	\end{equation}
59	where $A = (z^* \sigma / c)^{1/\beta}$ and $z^* = \Phi^{-1}(\pi^*)$ (justification in Appendix~\ref{app:alpha_c_proof}).
60	Cross-$T$ validation yields $\gamma \in [0.64, 2.08]$ (profile 95\% CI); the qualitative conclusion---detection improves with longer observation---is robust to the specific $\gamma$ value (cross-validation details and shape analysis in Appendix~\ref{app:alpha_c_proof}).
61	Dense $\alpha$ sampling favors a smooth sigmoid over a step function (Akaike information criterion difference $\Delta\mathrm{AIC}{=}-110$), but the fitted onset steepness ($k{=}15.7$) exceeds smooth-model predictions by $3$--$4{\times}$ (Appendix~\ref{app:power_sim}).
62	
```

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
5	\label{sec:setup}
6	
7	We study iterative training collapse using Pythia-410M~\citep{biderman2023pythia} as the primary architecture.
8	Training data is drawn from WikiText-103~\citep{merity2017pointer} following the replace paradigm of~\citet{shumailov2024curse}: each generation fine-tunes on a mixture of $\alpha$ synthetic and $(1{-}\alpha)$ real data, generates 5{,}000 replacement samples, and fully replaces the synthetic pool (hyperparameters in Appendix~\ref{app:training_details}).
9	We run $T{=}10$ generations across 13 contamination levels ($\alpha \in \{0.00, \ldots, 1.00\}$) with multi-seed replication, yielding ${\sim}1{,}500$ statistical tests after BH-FDR correction~\citep{benjamini1995controlling}.
10	We track two \emph{canary metrics} (token entropy and ECE) and three \emph{downstream metrics}: distinct-1, distinct-2~\citep{li2016diversity}, and perplexity (distinct-3: Appendix~\ref{app:distinct3}).
11	PermG uses 5{,}000 permutations on first-differenced series~\citep{shojaie2022granger} with maximum lag~5.
12	
13	
14	\subsection{Sharp Detection Onset}
15	\label{sec:regime}
16	
17	Diversity collapse occurs at every contamination level: distinct-1 declines by $14.1\%$ even at $\alpha{=}0.0$ (fine-tuning drift) and by $40.3{\pm}0.3\%$ at $\alpha{=}0.25$.
18	Despite this ubiquitous degradation, Granger detectability is sharply regime-dependent.
19	PermG finds 0/8 canary$\to$downstream pairs FDR-significant from $\alpha{=}0.00$ through $\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps to 6/8 (Clopper--Pearson 95\% CI $[0.25, 0.92]$) at $\alpha{=}0.50$, uniformly across all 3~fp32 seeds.
20	
21	This transition is striking because cumulative synthetic fraction barely changes across the boundary ($C{=}99.75\%$ at $\alpha{=}0.45$ vs.\ $99.90\%$ at $\alpha{=}0.50$), yet detection jumps from null to reliable.
22	Sub-threshold $p$-values at $\alpha{=}0.45$ ($p_{\mathrm{fdr}} = 0.059$--$0.091$) confirm proximity to the boundary.
23	Sigmoid regression yields $\alpha_c = 0.483 \pm 0.006$ ($R^2 = 0.969$; Figure~\ref{fig:hero}b); cross-seed consistency (3/3 seeds: $0/8{\to}6/8$) and specification-invariance across 1{,}160 configurations (Appendix~\ref{app:spec_curve}) argue for a genuine transition (Fisher exact: $p = 3.45 \times 10^{-11}$).
24	
25	\paragraph{Canary scoping.}
26	First-differencing scopes the canary claim precisely.
27	Canary$\to$diversity pairs retain unanimous FDR significance after first-differencing ($24/24 \to 24/24$ across 4~seeds at $\alpha{=}1.0$), while canary$\to$perplexity collapses entirely ($F \approx 0$, $p = 0.99$)~\citep{granger1974spurious}.
28	At $\alpha{=}1.0$, all 24 canary$\to$diversity pairs reach FDR significance in the forward direction with 0/24 reverse; entropy onset at generation~1 precedes distinct-$n$ at generation~2 in 16/18 WikiText conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:moment_cascade}).
29	
30	\paragraph{Blind zone.}
31	At $\alpha{=}0.10$, distinct-1 has already dropped by $39.6\%$, yet token entropy is indistinguishable from the null.
32	Below $\alpha_c$ at $T{=}10$, diversity collapse proceeds without any detectable Granger-precedence signal.
33	
34	\begin{figure}[t]
35	\centering
36	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/metric_trajectory.pdf}
37	\caption{Metric trajectories at $\alpha{=}1.0$ (Pythia-410M, 4~seeds). Token entropy drops at generation~1; distinct-1 declines from generations~2--3, with a consistent one-generation canary lead (shaded: inter-seed range).}
38	\label{fig:metric_trajectory}
39	\end{figure}
40	
41	\begin{figure}[t]
42	\centering
43	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_cascade_onset.pdf}
44	\caption{Cascade onset timeline (Pythia-410M, WikiText, fp32). Entropy and ECE reach onset at generation~1; distinct-$n$ follows at generation~2. The ordering $t_H \leq t_D$ holds across all conditions.}
45	\label{fig:cascade_onset}
46	\end{figure}
47	
48	\begin{figure}[t]
49	\centering
50	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_spec_curve.pdf}
51	\caption{Specification curve (1{,}160 configurations). The blind$\to$onset jump at $\alpha_c{\approx}0.50$ is robust across methods, pairs, and seeds.}
52	\label{fig:spec_curve}
53	\end{figure}
54	
55	
56	\subsection{Dose-Response and Detection Regimes}
57	\label{sec:dose_response}
58	
59	The PermG dose-response profile (Figure~\ref{fig:hero}b; Appendix~\ref{app:dose_response}) reveals three confirmed regimes---\emph{blind} ($\alpha \leq 0.45$, 0/8), \emph{onset} ($\alpha \approx 0.50$, 6/8), and \emph{detection} ($\alpha{\geq}0.75$, 6/8)---plus a non-monotonic anomaly at $\alpha \approx 0.60$ (0/8).
60	At this level, all direction-checked methods fail (PermG 0/8, threshold onset 0/8, Toda--Yamamoto 1/8), while bidirectional DiffXCorr maintains detection (6/8), suggesting compressed temporal lag disrupts directional separation.
61	The anomaly dissolves by $T{=}25$ ($5/8$), consistent with $\sqrt{T}$ power scaling (Appendix~\ref{app:t20_comparison}).
62	
63	
64	\subsection{Detection Boundary Left-Shift}
65	\label{sec:t20}
66	
67	Longer observation windows push the detection boundary leftward: $\alpha_c(5){\geq}1.0$, $\alpha_c(10){=}0.487$, consistent with power-law scaling (\S\ref{sec:theory}).
68	The $\alpha{=}0.45$ column validates this: undetectable at $T{\leq}10$ (0/8), it crosses into reliable detection at $T{=}15$ (6/8, dual-seed) and remains there at $T{=}20$ and $T{=}25$.
69	
70	\begin{figure}[t]
71	\centering
72	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_detection_surface.pdf}
73	\caption{Detection rate surface over $\alpha \times T$ (PermG forward, 3~seeds). Dashed curve: fitted boundary $\alpha_c(T)$. The blind zone ($\alpha \leq 0.45$) shrinks as $T$ increases.}
74	\label{fig:detection_surface}
75	\end{figure}
76	
77	
78	\subsection{Cross-Architecture Validation}
79	\label{sec:generalization}
80	
81	We evaluate PermG at $\alpha{=}1.0$ across 15 model-seed combinations spanning five families (345M--6.9B) and two domains (Table~\ref{tab:generalization}; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_arch_table}).
82	Forward detection reaches ${\geq}6/8$ in 11/15 conditions (Figure~\ref{fig:hero}d).
83	The four non-confirming conditions each isolate a distinct failure mode: precision sensitivity (GPT-2 bf16: 0/8 vs.\ fp32: 6/8), bidirectional confounding (GPT-2 s43), seed dependence (Pythia-1.4B: s42 2/8, s43 6/8), and entropy decoupling (SmolLM3-3B: 3/8).
84	Standalone PermG claims require architecture-specific null calibration (GPT-2 null FPR: 50\%; Appendix~\ref{app:fpr_independence}).
85	
86	
87	\subsection{Canary-Based Intervention (Proof of Concept)}
88	\label{sec:intervention}
89	
90	As a proof-of-concept under favorable conditions ($\alpha{=}1.0$), we implement predictive cessation: training switches to pure real data when token entropy drops ${>}15\%$ from generation~0 (robust band $[10\%, 22\%]$; Appendix~\ref{app:intervention_sensitivity}).
91	The predictive trigger fires at generation~1 (one generation before a reactive distinct-1 baseline), reducing peak perplexity by 64\% ($56.99$ vs.\ $157.09$; Figure~\ref{fig:intervention}) in both Pythia-410M and 1.4B.
92

98	\end{figure}
99

each generation fine-tunes on a mixture of $\\alpha$ synthetic and $(1{-}\\alpha)$ real data, generates 5{,}000 replacement samples, and fully replaces the synthetic pool (hyperparameters in lag~5.\n\n\n\\subsection{Sharp Detection Onset}\n\\label{sec:regime}\n\nDiversity collapse occurs at every contamination level: distinct-1 declines by $14.1\\%$ even at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (fine-tuning FDR-significant canary$\\to$diversity pairs across $\\alpha$ and $T$; dashed curve: fitted $\\alpha_c(T) \\propto T^{-\\gamma}$; $\\diamond$: non-monotonic anomaly; $\\star$: dissolution at $T{=}25$.}\n\\label{fig:phase_diagram}\n\\end{figure}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen language models train iteratively on their own output, the training distribution progressively loses its tail modes, a phenomenon termed model collapse~\\citep{shumailov2024curse,alemohammad2024mad}.\nThis process can be triggered by vanishingly small synthetic fractions~\\citep{dohmatob2025strong}: lexical diversity narrows, calibration deteriorates, and each generation inherits the biases of its predecessors.\nAs machine-generated text grows prevalent on the web, such feedback loops pose a growing risk to production pipelines, and by the time standard quality metrics register degradation, the distribution may have already shifted irreversibly.\n\nThis threat motivates a fundamental question: \\emph{can we reliably detect the onset of model collapse?}\nThe answer is less clear than it might appear.\nIterative training produces short, noisy time series (often fewer than 15 points) where detection power drops sharply~\\citep{boettiger2012limits}, and model collapse is a first-order transition~\\citep{truong2026phase}, structurally ruling out the critical-slowing-down early-warning signals that ecologists rely on~\\citep{scheffer2009early} (\\S\\ref{sec:related_work}).\nMore fundamentally, no prior work has systematically compared detection methods on this problem; as we show, the choice of statistical test alone can shift conclusions from universal detection to complete null on identical data.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[!t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.92\\textwidth]{figures/fig_composite_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Detection landscape overview (Pythia-410M, fp32, PermG).\n\\textbf{(a)}~Phase diagram: FDR-significant canary$\\to$diversity pairs across $\\alpha$ and $T$; dashed curve: fitted $\\alpha_c(T) \\propto T^{-\\gamma}$; $\\diamond$: non-monotonic anomaly; $\\star$: dissolution at $T{=}25$.\n\\textbf{(b)}~Dose-response with sigmoid fit ($\\alpha_c{=}0.483$); regime bands mark blind, onset, sync-dip, and detection zones.\n\\textbf{(c)}~Method sensitivity: five methods on identical data; PermG achieves 0\\% null-control FPR with 75\\% detection at $\\alpha{\\geq}0.50$.\n\\textbf{(d)}~Cross-architecture validation ($\\alpha{=}1.0$): forward detection (${\\geq}6/8$) in 11/15 conditions spanning five families (345M--6.9B).}\n\\label{fig:hero}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nWe systematically map this landscape on Pythia-410M~\\citep{biderman2023pythia} across 13 contamination levels with multi-seed replication (Figure~\\ref{fig:hero}).\nOur most consequential finding concerns method sensitivity: five statistical methods applied to identical data yield null-control FPR spanning 7\\% to 94\\% ($\\kappa{=}0.185$; Figure~\\ref{fig:hero}c), demonstrating that single-method analyses cannot reliably distinguish real signals from artifacts.\nPermutation Granger causality (PermG), the lowest-FPR method (7.4\\%, CI $[2.1\\%, 17.9\\%]$, 9~seeds), reveals structured terrain rather than smooth degradation: detection is absent through $\\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps from 0/8 to 6/8 significant pairs within a 2-percentage-point interval (Figure~\\ref{fig:hero}a), before a non-monotonic dip and eventual reliable detection above a power-law boundary $\\alpha_c(T)$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:alpha-c-theory}).\nCross-architecture evaluation at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ across five families (345M--6.9B) supports forward detection in 11/15 conditions, with four non-confirming cases isolating distinct failure modes (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\nOur canary claim is scoped to diversity metrics (distinct-$n$~\\citep{li2016diversity}); Granger precedence establishes temporal predictive improvement, not causation (\\S\\ref{sec:method}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{We document method sensitivity as a primary finding} (\\S\\ref{sec:mmct}).\n    Five methods yield null-control FPR from 7\\% to 94\\% ($\\kappa{=}0.185$); PermG, the lowest-FPR method across all five (7.4\\%, CI $[2.1\\%, 17.9\\%]$, 9~seeds), is selected as the primary detector.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{We map the detectability landscape} of iterative model collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:regime}--\\ref{sec:dose_response}).\n    Detection is absent through $\\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps from 0/8 to 6/8 within 2 percentage points; a detection boundary consistent with power-law scaling $\\alpha_c(T) \\propto T^{-\\gamma}$ ($\\gamma \\in [0.64, 2.08]$; Eq.~\\ref{eq:alpha-c-theory}) organizes three confirmed regimes (blind, onset, detection) plus one unexplained non-monotonic anomaly.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{We empirically validate a cascade ordering for canary metric selection} (\\S\\ref{sec:cascade}).\n    Under mode amplification, entropy responds before diversity by ${\\geq}1$ generation (Principles~\\ref{thm:entropy_leads}--\\ref{prop:div_lag}), confirmed in 14/15 experiments ($\\alpha \\geq 0.30$; GPT-2-medium s43 bidirectional).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

    "newString": "Empirically, 4/54 null-control rejections across nine seeds confirm adequate size control (7/9 seeds yield 0~FP; Appendix~\\ref{app:fpr_independence}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_method_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Method sensitivity: five methods yield dramatically different FDR-significant rates on identical data. PermG achieves 0\\% null-control FPR with 75\\% detection at $\\alpha{\\geq}0.50$; dashed line: 5\\% nominal.}\n\\label{fig:method_comparison}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Detection Boundary}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Cascade Ordering}\n\\label{sec:cascade}\n\nIf distributional collapse follows a predictable ordering across metrics, the earliest-responding metric can serve as a canary signal.\nWe formalize known metric properties---entropy as a continuous indicator of mode concentration, diversity as a threshold indicator of lexical coverage---under mode amplification~\\citep{shumailov2024curse,dohmatob2024tale} (derivations in Appendix~\\ref{app:cascade_theory}).\n\n\\begin{designprinciple}[Entropy responds before diversity]\\label{thm:entropy_leads}\nUnder mode amplification: \\textup{(a)} entropy can decrease while the $\\epsilon$-effective support $S_\\epsilon(p) {=} |\\{i: p_i > \\epsilon\\}|$ (hence distinct-$n$) is unchanged; \\textup{(b)} if distinct-$n$ decreases, entropy has already decreased.\n\\end{designprinciple}\n\nThe asymmetry arises because entropy responds to any mass redistribution (first-order), while diversity changes only when a token probability crosses the generation threshold~$\\epsilon$; the margin $\\gamma = \\min_{i: p_i > \\epsilon}(p_i - \\epsilon)$ quantifies how much shift diversity absorbs silently.\n\n\\begin{designprinciple}[Diversity lag $\\geq 1$ generation]\\label{prop:div_lag}\nIf the per-generation perturbation satisfies $\\alpha\\|\\mathcal{T}(p_t) - p_t\\|_\\infty < \\gamma(p_t, \\epsilon)$ through entropy onset $t_H$, then diversity onset $t_D \\geq t_H + 1$.\n\\end{designprinciple}\n\nECE co-moves with entropy at zero lag ($\\Delta\\mathrm{ECE} \\approx \\Delta C > 0$ whenever $\\Delta\\bar{H} < 0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cascade_theory}).\nThe predicted cascade $\\{H, \\mathrm{ECE}\\} \\to \\mathrm{Distinct}$-$n$ holds in 14/15 experiments at $\\alpha \\geq 0.30$: entropy and ECE onset at generation~1, distinct-$n$ at generation~2 ($t_H{=}t_{\\mathrm{ECE}}{=}1$, $t_D{=}2$ uniformly; \\S\\ref{sec:regime}; Appendix~\\ref{app:moment_cascade}).\nThe assumption requires monotonic entropy decline; SmolLM3-3B (Table~\\ref{tab:generalization}) provides an empirical counter-example.\n\n\\subsection{Design Considerations}\n\\label{sec:pitfalls}\n\nWe use Granger causality~\\citep{granger1969investigating} to test temporal precedence---whether past canary values improve prediction of downstream metrics---not mechanistic causation; bivariate Granger tests cannot exclude confounders~\\citep{shojaie2022granger}.\nLead-lag analysis in iterative model collapse is fragile along three axes:\n\\emph{Method sensitivity}: five detection methods applied to identical data yield forward significance rates spanning 0\\% to 92\\%, with $\\kappa{=}0.185$.\n\\emph{Spurious regression}: first-differencing eliminates canary$\\to$perplexity associations ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$) while preserving canary$\\to$diversity (24/24$\\to$24/24)~\\citep{granger1974spurious}.\n\\emph{Regime dependence}: the same Granger test shifts from 0/8 to 6/8 significant pairs within a 2-percentage-point interval.\n\n\\subsection{Method Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:mmct}\n\nWe compare five lead-lag detection methods---naive cross-correlation (NaiveXCorr), differenced cross-correlation (DiffXCorr), threshold onset, permutation Granger causality (PermG), and Toda--Yamamoto~\\citep{toda1995statistical}---in a multi-method comparison~\\citep{simonsohn2020specification} (algorithm in Appendix~\\ref{app:mmct}), calibrated against null-control ($\\alpha{=}0.0$) data.\nPooled null-control FPR across nine seeds (54 pairs): PermG 7.4\\%, DiffXCorr 14.8\\%, Threshold 24.1\\%, Toda--Yamamoto 33.3\\%, NaiveXCorr 94.4\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:fpr_independence}).\nPermG (4/54; 95\\% CI $[2.1\\%, 17.9\\%]$) achieves the lowest FPR, combining directional separation with consistent detection at $\\alpha \\geq 0.50$ (Shapley analysis: Appendix~\\ref{app:shapley}).\nDetection results use equal-weight majority voting ($K{\\geq}3$ of 5 methods) with forward-direction filtering; $K{=}3$ yields 11.1\\% FPR with 75\\% true positive rate (TPR) at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (Table~\\ref{tab:mmct_k_operating}).\n\n\\paragraph{Permutation Granger causality.}\nPermG tests whether past values of canary metric $X$ improve prediction of downstream metric $Y$ beyond $Y$'s own history~\\citep{shojaie2022granger}, using 5{,}000 permutations~\\citep{phipson2010permutation} on first-differenced series with Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) FDR correction at $q{=}0.05$ across 8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs.\nThe permutation null controls Type~I error by construction; BH-FDR remains valid under positive regression dependence on a subset (PRDS)~\\citep{benjamini2001control}.\nEmpirically, 4/54 null-control rejections across nine seeds confirm adequate size control (7/9 seeds yield 0~FP; Appendix~\\ref{app:fpr_independence}).\n\n\\subsection{Detection Boundary}\n\\label{sec:theory}\n\nThe detection boundary is consistent with power-law scaling in observation length:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\alpha_c(T) = A \\cdot T^{-\\gamma},\n\\label{eq:alpha-c}\n\\end{equation}\nUnder an empirical sub-linear signal model $S(\\alpha) = c \\cdot \\alpha^\\beta$ ($\\beta < 1$), the exponent satisfies $\\gamma = 1/(2\\beta)$:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\alpha_c(T) = A \\cdot T^{-1/(2\\beta)},\n\\label{eq:alpha-c-theory}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $A = (z^* \\sigma / c)^{1/\\beta}$ and $z^* = \\Phi^{-1}(\\pi^*)$ (justification in Appendix~\\ref{app:alpha_c_proof}).\nCross-$T$ validation yields $\\gamma \\in [0.64, 2.08]$ (profile 95\\% CI); the qualitative conclusion---detection improves with longer observation---is robust to the specific $\\gamma$ value (cross-validation details and shape analysis in Appendix~\\ref{app:alpha_c_proof}).\nDense $\\alpha$ sampling favors a smooth sigmoid over a step function (Akaike information criterion difference $\\Delta\\mathrm{AIC}{=}-110$), but the fitted onset steepness ($k{=}15.7$) exceeds smooth-model predictions by $3$--$4{\\times}$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:power_sim}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nWe study iterative training collapse using each generation fine-tunes on a mixture of $\\alpha$ synthetic and $(1{-}\\alpha)$ real data, generates 5{,}000 replacement samples, and fully replaces the synthetic pool (hyperparameters in Appendix~\\ref{app:training_details}).\nWe run $T{=}10$ generations across 13 contamination levels ($\\alpha \\in \\{0.00, \\ldots, 1.00\\}$) with multi-seed replication, yielding ${\\sim}1{,}500$ statistical tests after BH-FDR correction~\\citep{benjamini1995controlling}.\nWe track two \\emph{canary metrics} (token entropy and ECE) and three \\emph{downstream metrics}: distinct-1, distinct-2~\\citep{li2016diversity}, and perplexity (distinct-3: Appendix~\\ref{app:distinct3}).\nPermG uses 5{,}000 permutations on first-differenced series~\\citep{shojaie2022granger} with maximum lag~5.\n\n\n\\subsection{Sharp Detection Onset}\n\\label{sec:regime}\n\nDiversity collapse occurs at every contamination level: distinct-1 declines by $14.1\\%$ even at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (fine-tuning drift) and by $40.3{\\pm}0.3\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.25$.\nDespite this ubiquitous degradation, Granger detectability is sharply regime-dependent.\nPermG finds 0/8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs FDR-significant from $\\alpha{=}0.00$ through $\\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps to 6/8 (Clopper--Pearson 95\\% CI $[0.25, 0.92]$) at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, uniformly across all 3~fp32 seeds.\n\nThis transition is striking because cumulative synthetic fraction barely changes across the boundary ($C{=}99.75\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.45$ vs.\\ $99.90\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.50$), yet detection jumps from null to reliable.\nSub-threshold $p$-values at $\\alpha{=}0.45$ ($p_{\\mathrm{fdr}} = 0.059$--$0.091$) confirm proximity to the boundary.\nSigmoid regression yields $\\alpha_c = 0.483 \\pm 0.006$ ($R^2 = 0.969$; Figure~\\ref{fig:hero}b); cross-seed consistency (3/3 seeds: $0/8{\\to}6/8$) and specification-invariance across 1{,}160 configurations (Appendix~\\ref{app:spec_curve}) argue for a genuine transition (Fisher exact: $p = 3.45 \\times 10^{-11}$).\n\n\\paragraph{Canary scoping.}\nFirst-differencing scopes the canary claim precisely.\nCanary$\\to$diversity pairs retain unanimous FDR significance after first-differencing ($24/24 \\to 24/24$ across 4~seeds at $\\alpha{=}1.0$), while canary$\\to$perplexity collapses entirely ($F \\approx 0$, $p = 0.99$)~\\citep{granger1974spurious}.\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$, all 24 canary$\\to$diversity pairs reach FDR significance in the forward direction with 0/24 reverse; entropy onset at generation~1 precedes distinct-$n$ at generation~2 in 16/18 WikiText conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:moment_cascade}).\n\n\\paragraph{Blind zone.}\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.10$, distinct-1 has already dropped by $39.6\\%$, yet token entropy is indistinguishable from the null.\nBelow $\\alpha_c$ at $T{=}10$, diversity collapse proceeds without any detectable Granger-precedence signal.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/metric_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Metric trajectories at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (Pythia-410M, 4~seeds). Token entropy drops at generation~1; distinct-1 declines from generations~2--3, with a consistent one-generation canary lead (shaded: inter-seed range).}\n\\label{fig:metric_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_cascade_onset.pdf}\n\\caption{Cascade onset timeline (Pythia-410M, WikiText, fp32). Entropy and ECE reach onset at generation~1; distinct-$n$ follows at generation~2. The ordering $t_H \\leq t_D$ holds across all conditions.}\n\\label{fig:cascade_onset}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_spec_curve.pdf}\n\\caption{Specification curve (1{,}160 configurations). The blind$\\to$onset jump at $\\alpha_c{\\approx}0.50$ is robust across methods, pairs, and seeds.}\n\\label{fig:spec_curve}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response and Detection Regimes}\n\\label{sec:dose_response}\n\nThe PermG dose-response profile (Figure~\\ref{fig:hero}b; Appendix~\\ref{app:dose_response}) reveals three confirmed regimes---\\emph{blind} ($\\alpha \\leq 0.45$, 0/8), \\emph{onset} ($\\alpha \\approx 0.50$, 6/8), and \\emph{detection} ($\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$, 6/8)---plus a non-monotonic anomaly at $\\alpha \\approx 0.60$ (0/8).\nAt this level, all direction-checked methods fail (PermG 0/8, threshold onset 0/8, Toda--Yamamoto 1/8), while bidirectional DiffXCorr maintains detection (6/8), suggesting compressed temporal lag disrupts directional separation.\nThe anomaly dissolves by $T{=}25$ ($5/8$), consistent with $\\sqrt{T}$ power scaling (Appendix~\\ref{app:t20_comparison}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Detection Boundary Left-Shift}\n\\label{sec:t20}\n\nLonger observation windows push the detection boundary leftward: $\\alpha_c(5){\\geq}1.0$, $\\alpha_c(10){=}0.487$, consistent with power-law scaling (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}).\nThe $\\alpha{=}0.45$ column validates this: undetectable at $T{\\leq}10$ (0/8), it crosses into reliable detection at $T{=}15$ (6/8, dual-seed) and remains there at $T{=}20$ and $T{=}25$.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_detection_surface.pdf}\n\\caption{Detection rate surface over $\\alpha \\times T$ (PermG forward, 3~seeds). Dashed curve: fitted boundary $\\alpha_c(T)$. The blind zone ($\\alpha \\leq 0.45$) shrinks as $T$ increases.}\n\\label{fig:detection_surface}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Architecture Validation}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nWe evaluate PermG at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ across 15 model-seed combinations spanning five families (345M--6.9B) and two domains (Table~\\ref{tab:generalization}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_arch_table}).\nForward detection reaches ${\\geq}6/8$ in 11/15 conditions (Figure~\\ref{fig:hero}d).\nThe four non-confirming conditions each isolate a distinct failure mode: precision sensitivity (GPT-2 bf16: 0/8 vs.\\ fp32: 6/8), bidirectional confounding (GPT-2 s43), seed dependence (Pythia-1.4B: s42 2/8, s43 6/8), and entropy decoupling (SmolLM3-3B: 3/8).\nStandalone PermG claims require architecture-specific null calibration (GPT-2 null FPR: 50\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:fpr_independence}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Canary-Based Intervention (Proof of Concept)}\n\\label{sec:intervention}\n\nAs a proof-of-concept under favorable conditions ($\\alpha{=}1.0$), we implement predictive cessation: training switches to pure real data when token entropy drops ${>}15\\%$ from generation~0 (robust band $[10\\%, 22\\%]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:intervention_sensitivity}).\nThe predictive trigger fires at generation~1 (one generation before a reactive distinct-1 baseline), reducing peak perplexity by 64\\% ($56.99$ vs.\\ $157.09$; Figure~\\ref{fig:intervention}) in both Pythia-410M and 1.4B.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_intervention.pdf}\n\\caption{Canary-triggered intervention (Pythia-410M, $\\alpha{=}1.0$). Predictive cessation on entropy (generation~1) reduces peak perplexity by 64\\% versus reactive triggering on distinct-1 (generation~2).}\n\\label{fig:intervention}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Detection outcomes across contamination levels (Pythia-410M, seed~42, $T{=}10$, fp32). PermG: forward FDR-significant pairs; $K{\\geq}3$: consensus pairs (of 8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs).}\n\\label{tab:summary_detection}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$\\alpha$ & PermG & $K{\\geq}3$ & Regime \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.00 & 0/8 & 0/8 & null \\\\\n0.25 & 0/8 & 0/8 & blind \\\\\n0.45 & 0/8 & 0/8 & blind \\\\\n0.50 & 6/8 & 6/8 & onset \\\\\n0.60 & 0/8 & 0/8 & anomaly \\\\\n0.75 & 6/8 & 6/8 & detection \\\\\n1.00 & 6/8 & 6/8 & detection \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Consensus threshold operating characteristics ($K \\in \\{2,3,4,5\\}$). FPR: null-control rate (9 seeds, 54 pairs); TPR: detection rate at selected $\\alpha$ levels (Pythia-410M, $T{=}10$, fp32). $K{=}3$ balances FPR control (11.1\\%) against detection power (75\\% at $\\alpha{=}1.0$); $K{=}4$ eliminates false positives but reduces TPR to ${\\leq}25\\%$.}\n\\label{tab:mmct_k_operating}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& $K{\\geq}2$ & $K{\\geq}3$ & $K{\\geq}4$ & $K{=}5$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nFPR ($\\alpha{=}0.0$) & 38.9\\% & 11.1\\% & 0\\% & 0\\% \\\\\nTPR ($\\alpha{=}0.50$) & 85.4\\% & 66.7\\% & 25.0\\% & 4.2\\% \\\\\nTPR ($\\alpha{=}0.75$) & 72.9\\% & 37.5\\% & 0\\% & 0\\% \\\\\nTPR ($\\alpha{=}1.0$) & 75.0\\% & 75.0\\% & 25.0\\% & 0\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n",

    "newString": "The anomaly dissolves by $T{=}25$ ($5/8$), consistent with $\\sqrt{T}$ power scaling (Appendix~\\ref{app:t20_comparison}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Dose-response: PermG detection rate with sigmoid fit ($\\alpha_c{=}0.483$; shaded 95\\% CI). Regime bands mark blind, onset, sync-dip, and detection zones.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Detection Boundary Left-Shift}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nWe study iterative training collapse using Pythia-410M~\\citep{biderman2023pythia} as the primary architecture.\nTraining data is drawn from WikiText-103~\\citep{merity2017pointer} following the replace paradigm of~\\citet{shumailov2024curse}: each generation fine-tunes on a mixture of $\\alpha$ synthetic and $(1{-}\\alpha)$ real data, generates 5{,}000 replacement samples, and fully replaces the synthetic pool (hyperparameters in Appendix~\\ref{app:training_details}).\nWe run $T{=}10$ generations across 13 contamination levels ($\\alpha \\in \\{0.00, \\ldots, 1.00\\}$) with multi-seed replication, yielding ${\\sim}1{,}500$ statistical tests after BH-FDR correction~\\citep{benjamini1995controlling}.\nWe track two \\emph{canary metrics} (token entropy and ECE) and three \\emph{downstream metrics}: distinct-1, distinct-2~\\citep{li2016diversity}, and perplexity (distinct-3: Appendix~\\ref{app:distinct3}).\nPermG uses 5{,}000 permutations on first-differenced series~\\citep{shojaie2022granger} with maximum lag~5.\n\n\n\\subsection{Sharp Detection Onset}\n\\label{sec:regime}\n\nDiversity collapse occurs at every contamination level: distinct-1 declines by $14.1\\%$ even at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (fine-tuning drift) and by $40.3{\\pm}0.3\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.25$.\nDespite this ubiquitous degradation, Granger detectability is sharply regime-dependent.\nPermG finds 0/8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs FDR-significant from $\\alpha{=}0.00$ through $\\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps to 6/8 (Clopper--Pearson 95\\% CI $[0.25, 0.92]$) at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, uniformly across all 3~fp32 seeds.\n\nThis transition is striking because cumulative synthetic fraction barely changes across the boundary ($C{=}99.75\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.45$ vs.\\ $99.90\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.50$), yet detection jumps from null to reliable.\nSub-threshold $p$-values at $\\alpha{=}0.45$ ($p_{\\mathrm{fdr}} = 0.059$--$0.091$) confirm proximity to the boundary.\nSigmoid regression yields $\\alpha_c = 0.483 \\pm 0.006$ ($R^2 = 0.969$; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}); cross-seed consistency (3/3 seeds: $0/8{\\to}6/8$) and specification-invariance across 1{,}160 configurations (Appendix~\\ref{app:spec_curve}) argue for a genuine transition (Fisher exact: $p = 3.45 \\times 10^{-11}$).\n\n\\paragraph{Canary scoping.}\nFirst-differencing scopes the canary claim precisely.\nCanary$\\to$diversity pairs retain unanimous FDR significance after first-differencing ($24/24 \\to 24/24$ across 4~seeds at $\\alpha{=}1.0$), while canary$\\to$perplexity collapses entirely ($F \\approx 0$, $p = 0.99$)~\\citep{granger1974spurious}.\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$, all 24 canary$\\to$diversity pairs reach FDR significance in the forward direction with 0/24 reverse; entropy onset at generation~1 precedes distinct-$n$ at generation~2 in 16/18 WikiText conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:moment_cascade}).\n\n\\paragraph{Blind zone.}\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.10$, distinct-1 has already dropped by $39.6\\%$, yet token entropy is indistinguishable from the null.\nBelow $\\alpha_c$ at $T{=}10$, diversity collapse proceeds without any detectable Granger-precedence signal.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/metric_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Metric trajectories at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (Pythia-410M, 4~seeds). Token entropy drops at generation~1; distinct-1 declines from generations~2--3, with a consistent one-generation canary lead (shaded: inter-seed range).}\n\\label{fig:metric_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_cascade_onset.pdf}\n\\caption{Cascade onset timeline (Pythia-410M, WikiText, fp32). Entropy and ECE reach onset at generation~1; distinct-$n$ follows at generation~2. The ordering $t_H \\leq t_D$ holds across all conditions.}\n\\label{fig:cascade_onset}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_spec_curve.pdf}\n\\caption{Specification curve (1{,}160 configurations). The blind$\\to$onset jump at $\\alpha_c{\\approx}0.50$ is robust across methods, pairs, and seeds.}\n\\label{fig:spec_curve}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response and Detection Regimes}\n\\label{sec:dose_response}\n\nThe PermG dose-response profile (Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}; Appendix~\\ref{app:dose_response}) reveals three confirmed regimes---\\emph{blind} ($\\alpha \\leq 0.45$, 0/8), \\emph{onset} ($\\alpha \\approx 0.50$, 6/8), and \\emph{detection} ($\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$, 6/8)---plus a non-monotonic anomaly at $\\alpha \\approx 0.60$ (0/8).\nAt this level, all direction-checked methods fail (PermG 0/8, threshold onset 0/8, Toda--Yamamoto 1/8), while bidirectional DiffXCorr maintains detection (6/8), suggesting compressed temporal lag disrupts directional separation.\nThe anomaly dissolves by $T{=}25$ ($5/8$), consistent with $\\sqrt{T}$ power scaling (Appendix~\\ref{app:t20_comparison}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Detection Boundary Left-Shift}\n\\label{sec:t20}\n\nLonger observation windows push the detection boundary leftward: $\\alpha_c(5){\\geq}1.0$, $\\alpha_c(10){=}0.487$, consistent with power-law scaling (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}).\nThe $\\alpha{=}0.45$ column validates this: undetectable at $T{\\leq}10$ (0/8), it crosses into reliable detection at $T{=}15$ (6/8, dual-seed) and remains there at $T{=}20$ and $T{=}25$.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_detection_surface.pdf}\n\\caption{Detection rate surface over $\\alpha \\times T$ (PermG forward, 3~seeds). Dashed curve: fitted boundary $\\alpha_c(T)$. The blind zone ($\\alpha \\leq 0.45$) shrinks as $T$ increases.}\n\\label{fig:detection_surface}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Architecture Validation}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nWe evaluate PermG at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ across 15 model-seed combinations spanning five families (345M--6.9B) and two domains (Table~\\ref{tab:generalization}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_arch_table}).\nForward detection reaches ${\\geq}6/8$ in 11/15 conditions (Figure~\\ref{fig:hero}d).\nThe four non-confirming conditions each isolate a distinct failure mode: precision sensitivity (GPT-2 bf16: 0/8 vs.\\ fp32: 6/8), bidirectional confounding (GPT-2 s43), seed dependence (Pythia-1.4B: s42 2/8, s43 6/8), and entropy decoupling (SmolLM3-3B: 3/8).\nStandalone PermG claims require architecture-specific null calibration (GPT-2 null FPR: 50\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:fpr_independence}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Canary-Based Intervention (Proof of Concept)}\n\\label{sec:intervention}\n\nAs a proof-of-concept under favorable conditions ($\\alpha{=}1.0$), we implement predictive cessation: training switches to pure real data when token entropy drops ${>}15\\%$ from generation~0 (robust band $[10\\%, 22\\%]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:intervention_sensitivity}).\nThe predictive trigger fires at generation~1 (one generation before a reactive distinct-1 baseline), reducing peak perplexity by 64\\% ($56.99$ vs.\\ $157.09$; Figure~\\ref{fig:intervention}) in both Pythia-410M and 1.4B.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_intervention.pdf}\n\\caption{Canary-triggered intervention (Pythia-410M, $\\alpha{=}1.0$). Predictive cessation on entropy (generation~1) reduces peak perplexity by 64\\% versus reactive triggering on distinct-1 (generation~2).}\n\\label{fig:intervention}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Detection outcomes across contamination levels (Pythia-410M, seed~42, $T{=}10$, fp32). PermG: forward FDR-significant pairs; $K{\\geq}3$: consensus pairs (of 8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs).}\n\\label{tab:summary_detection}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$\\alpha$ & PermG & $K{\\geq}3$ & Regime \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.00 & 0/8 & 0/8 & null \\\\\n0.25 & 0/8 & 0/8 & blind \\\\\n0.45 & 0/8 & 0/8 & blind \\\\\n0.50 & 6/8 & 6/8 & onset \\\\\n0.60 & 0/8 & 0/8 & anomaly \\\\\n0.75 & 6/8 & 6/8 & detection \\\\\n1.00 & 6/8 & 6/8 & detection \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Consensus threshold operating characteristics ($K \\in \\{2,3,4,5\\}$). FPR: null-control rate (9 seeds, 54 pairs); TPR: detection rate at selected $\\alpha$ levels (Pythia-410M, $T{=}10$, fp32). $K{=}3$ balances FPR control (11.1\\%) against detection power (75\\% at $\\alpha{=}1.0$); $K{=}4$ eliminates false positives but reduces TPR to ${\\leq}25\\%$.}\n\\label{tab:mmct_k_operating}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& $K{\\geq}2$ & $K{\\geq}3$ & $K{\\geq}4$ & $K{=}5$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nFPR ($\\alpha{=}0.0$) & 38.9\\% & 11.1\\% & 0\\% & 0\\% \\\\\nTPR ($\\alpha{=}0.50$) & 85.4\\% & 66.7\\% & 25.0\\% & 4.2\\% \\\\\nTPR ($\\alpha{=}0.75$) & 72.9\\% & 37.5\\% & 0\\% & 0\\% \\\\\nTPR ($\\alpha{=}1.0$) & 75.0\\% & 75.0\\% & 25.0\\% & 0\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nWe study iterative training collapse using Pythia-410M~\\citep{biderman2023pythia} as the primary architecture.\nTraining data is drawn from WikiText-103~\\citep{merity2017pointer} following the replace paradigm of~\\citet{shumailov2024curse}: each generation fine-tunes on a mixture of $\\alpha$ synthetic and $(1{-}\\alpha)$ real data, generates 5{,}000 replacement samples, and fully replaces the synthetic pool (hyperparameters in Appendix~\\ref{app:training_details}).\nWe run $T{=}10$ generations across 13 contamination levels ($\\alpha \\in \\{0.00, \\ldots, 1.00\\}$) with multi-seed replication, yielding ${\\sim}1{,}500$ statistical tests after BH-FDR correction~\\citep{benjamini1995controlling}.\nWe track two \\emph{canary metrics} (token entropy and ECE) and three \\emph{downstream metrics}: distinct-1, distinct-2~\\citep{li2016diversity}, and perplexity (distinct-3: Appendix~\\ref{app:distinct3}).\nPermG uses 5{,}000 permutations on first-differenced series~\\citep{shojaie2022granger} with maximum lag~5.\n\n\n\\subsection{Sharp Detection Onset}\n\\label{sec:regime}\n\nDiversity collapse occurs at every contamination level: distinct-1 declines by $14.1\\%$ even at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (fine-tuning drift) and by $40.3{\\pm}0.3\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.25$.\nDespite this ubiquitous degradation, Granger detectability is sharply regime-dependent.\nPermG finds 0/8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs FDR-significant from $\\alpha{=}0.00$ through $\\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps to 6/8 (Clopper--Pearson 95\\% CI $[0.25, 0.92]$) at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, uniformly across all 3~fp32 seeds.\n\nThis transition is striking because cumulative synthetic fraction barely changes across the boundary ($C{=}99.75\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.45$ vs.\\ $99.90\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.50$), yet detection jumps from null to reliable.\nSub-threshold $p$-values at $\\alpha{=}0.45$ ($p_{\\mathrm{fdr}} = 0.059$--$0.091$) confirm proximity to the boundary.\nSigmoid regression yields $\\alpha_c = 0.483 \\pm 0.006$ ($R^2 = 0.969$; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}); cross-seed consistency (3/3 seeds: $0/8{\\to}6/8$) and specification-invariance across 1{,}160 configurations (Appendix~\\ref{app:spec_curve}) argue for a genuine transition (Fisher exact: $p = 3.45 \\times 10^{-11}$).\n\n\\paragraph{Canary scoping.}\nFirst-differencing scopes the canary claim precisely.\nCanary$\\to$diversity pairs retain unanimous FDR significance after first-differencing ($24/24 \\to 24/24$ across 4~seeds at $\\alpha{=}1.0$), while canary$\\to$perplexity collapses entirely ($F \\approx 0$, $p = 0.99$)~\\citep{granger1974spurious}.\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$, all 24 canary$\\to$diversity pairs reach FDR significance in the forward direction with 0/24 reverse; entropy onset at generation~1 precedes distinct-$n$ at generation~2 in 16/18 WikiText conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:moment_cascade}).\n\n\\paragraph{Blind zone.}\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.10$, distinct-1 has already dropped by $39.6\\%$, yet token entropy is indistinguishable from the null.\nBelow $\\alpha_c$ at $T{=}10$, diversity collapse proceeds without any detectable Granger-precedence signal.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/metric_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Metric trajectories at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (Pythia-410M, 4~seeds). Token entropy drops at generation~1; distinct-1 declines from generations~2--3, with a consistent one-generation canary lead (shaded: inter-seed range).}\n\\label{fig:metric_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_cascade_onset.pdf}\n\\caption{Cascade onset timeline (Pythia-410M, WikiText, fp32). Entropy and ECE reach onset at generation~1; distinct-$n$ follows at generation~2. The ordering $t_H \\leq t_D$ holds across all conditions.}\n\\label{fig:cascade_onset}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_spec_curve.pdf}\n\\caption{Specification curve (1{,}160 configurations). The blind$\\to$onset jump at $\\alpha_c{\\approx}0.50$ is robust across methods, pairs, and seeds.}\n\\label{fig:spec_curve}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response and Detection Regimes}\n\\label{sec:dose_response}\n\nThe PermG dose-response profile (Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}; Appendix~\\ref{app:dose_response}) reveals three confirmed regimes---\\emph{blind} ($\\alpha \\leq 0.45$, 0/8), \\emph{onset} ($\\alpha \\approx 0.50$, 6/8), and \\emph{detection} ($\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$, 6/8)---plus a non-monotonic anomaly at $\\alpha \\approx 0.60$ (0/8).\nAt this level, all direction-checked methods fail (PermG 0/8, threshold onset 0/8, Toda--Yamamoto 1/8), while bidirectional DiffXCorr maintains detection (6/8), suggesting compressed temporal lag disrupts directional separation.\nThe anomaly dissolves by $T{=}25$ ($5/8$), consistent with $\\sqrt{T}$ power scaling (Appendix~\\ref{app:t20_comparison}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Dose-response: PermG detection rate with sigmoid fit ($\\alpha_c{=}0.483$; shaded 95\\% CI). Regime bands mark blind, onset, sync-dip, and detection zones.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Detection Boundary Left-Shift}\n\\label{sec:t20}\n\nLonger observation windows push the detection boundary leftward: $\\alpha_c(5){\\geq}1.0$, $\\alpha_c(10){=}0.487$, consistent with power-law scaling (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}).\nThe $\\alpha{=}0.45$ column validates this: undetectable at $T{\\leq}10$ (0/8), it crosses into reliable detection at $T{=}15$ (6/8, dual-seed) and remains there at $T{=}20$ and $T{=}25$.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_detection_surface.pdf}\n\\caption{Detection rate surface over $\\alpha \\times T$ (PermG forward, 3~seeds). Dashed curve: fitted boundary $\\alpha_c(T)$. The blind zone ($\\alpha \\leq 0.45$) shrinks as $T$ increases.}\n\\label{fig:detection_surface}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Architecture Validation}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nWe evaluate PermG at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ across 15 model-seed combinations spanning five families (345M--6.9B) and two domains (Table~\\ref{tab:generalization}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_arch_table}).\nForward detection reaches ${\\geq}6/8$ in 11/15 conditions (Figure~\\ref{fig:hero}d).\nThe four non-confirming conditions each isolate a distinct failure mode: precision sensitivity (GPT-2 bf16: 0/8 vs.\\ fp32: 6/8), bidirectional confounding (GPT-2 s43), seed dependence (Pythia-1.4B: s42 2/8, s43 6/8), and entropy decoupling (SmolLM3-3B: 3/8).\nStandalone PermG claims require architecture-specific null calibration (GPT-2 null FPR: 50\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:fpr_independence}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Canary-Based Intervention (Proof of Concept)}\n\\label{sec:intervention}\n\nAs a proof-of-concept under favorable conditions ($\\alpha{=}1.0$), we implement predictive cessation: training switches to pure real data when token entropy drops ${>}15\\%$ from generation~0 (robust band $[10\\%, 22\\%]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:intervention_sensitivity}).\nThe predictive trigger fires at generation~1 (one generation before a reactive distinct-1 baseline), reducing peak perplexity by 64\\% ($56.99$ vs.\\ $157.09$; Figure~\\ref{fig:intervention}) in both Pythia-410M and 1.4B.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_intervention.pdf}\n\\caption{Canary-triggered intervention (Pythia-410M, $\\alpha{=}1.0$). Predictive cessation on entropy (generation~1) reduces peak perplexity by 64\\% versus reactive triggering on distinct-1 (generation~2).}\n\\label{fig:intervention}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Detection outcomes across contamination levels (Pythia-410M, seed~42, $T{=}10$, fp32). PermG: forward FDR-significant pairs; $K{\\geq}3$: consensus pairs (of 8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs).}\n\\label{tab:summary_detection}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$\\alpha$ & PermG & $K{\\geq}3$ & Regime \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.00 & 0/8 & 0/8 & null \\\\\n0.25 & 0/8 & 0/8 & blind \\\\\n0.45 & 0/8 & 0/8 & blind \\\\\n0.50 & 6/8 & 6/8 & onset \\\\\n0.60 & 0/8 & 0/8 & anomaly \\\\\n0.75 & 6/8 & 6/8 & detection \\\\\n1.00 & 6/8 & 6/8 & detection \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Consensus threshold operating characteristics ($K \\in \\{2,3,4,5\\}$). FPR: null-control rate (9 seeds, 54 pairs); TPR: detection rate at selected $\\alpha$ levels (Pythia-410M, $T{=}10$, fp32). $K{=}3$ balances FPR control (11.1\\%) against detection power (75\\% at $\\alpha{=}1.0$); $K{=}4$ eliminates false positives but reduces TPR to ${\\leq}25\\%$.}\n\\label{tab:mmct_k_operating}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& $K{\\geq}2$ & $K{\\geq}3$ & $K{\\geq}4$ & $K{=}5$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nFPR ($\\alpha{=}0.0$) & 38.9\\% & 11.1\\% & 0\\% & 0\\% \\\\\nTPR ($\\alpha{=}0.50$) & 85.4\\% & 66.7\\% & 25.0\\% & 4.2\\% \\\\\nTPR ($\\alpha{=}0.75$) & 72.9\\% & 37.5\\% & 0\\% & 0\\% \\\\\nTPR ($\\alpha{=}1.0$) & 75.0\\% & 75.0\\% & 25.0\\% & 0\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n",

The blind$\\to$onset jump at $\\alpha_c{\\approx}0.50$ is robust across methods, pairs, and seeds.}\n\\label{fig:spec_curve}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response and Detection Regimes}\n\\label{sec:dose_response}\n\nThe PermG dose-response profile (Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}; Appendix~\\ref{app:dose_response}) reveals three confirmed regimes---\\emph{blind} ($\\alpha \\leq 0.45$, 0/8), \\emph{onset} ($\\alpha \\approx 0.50$, 6/8), and \\emph{detection} ($\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$, 6/8)---plus a non-monotonic anomaly at $\\alpha \\approx 0.60$ (0/8).\nAt this level, all direction-checked methods fail (PermG 0/8, threshold onset 0/8, Toda--Yamamoto 1/8), while bidirectional DiffXCorr maintains detection (6/8), suggesting compressed temporal lag disrupts directional separation.\nThe anomaly dissolves by $T{=}25$ ($5/8$), consistent with $\\sqrt{T}$ power scaling (Appendix~\\ref{app:t20_comparison}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Dose-response: PermG detection rate with sigmoid fit ($\\alpha_c{=}0.483$; shaded 95\\% CI). Regime bands mark blind, onset, sync-dip, and detection zones.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Detection Boundary Left-Shift}\n\\label{sec:t20}\n\nLonger observation windows push the detection boundary leftward: $\\alpha_c(5){\\geq}1.0$, $\\alpha_c(10){=}0.487$, consistent with power-law scaling (\\S\\ref{sec:theory}).\nThe $\\alpha{=}0.45$ column validates this: undetectable at $T{\\leq}10$ (0/8), it crosses into reliable detection at $T{=}15$ (6/8, dual-seed) and remains there at $T{=}20$ and $T{=}25$.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_detection_surface.pdf}\n\\caption{Detection rate surface over $\\alpha \\times T$ (PermG forward, 3~seeds). Dashed curve: fitted boundary $\\alpha_c(T)$. The blind zone ($\\alpha \\leq 0.45$) shrinks as $T$ increases.}\n\\label{fig:detection_surface}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Architecture Validation}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nWe evaluate PermG at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ across 15 model-seed combinations spanning five families (345M--6.9B) and two domains (Table~\\ref{tab:generalization}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_arch_table}).\nForward detection reaches ${\\geq}6/8$ in 11/15 conditions (Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_arch_heatmap}).\nThe four non-confirming conditions each isolate a distinct failure mode: precision sensitivity (GPT-2 bf16: 0/8 vs.\\ fp32: 6/8), bidirectional confounding (GPT-2 s43), seed dependence (Pythia-1.4B: s42 2/8, s43 6/8), and entropy decoupling (SmolLM3-3B: 3/8).\nStandalone PermG claims require architecture-specific null calibration (GPT-2 null FPR: 50\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:fpr_independence}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Canary-Based Intervention (Proof of Concept)}\n\\label{sec:intervention}\n\nAs a proof-of-concept under favorable conditions ($\\alpha{=}1.0$), we implement predictive cessation: training switches to pure real data when token entropy drops ${>}15\\%$ from generation~0 (robust band $[10\\%, 22\\%]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:intervention_sensitivity}).\nThe predictive trigger fires at generation~1 (one generation before a reactive distinct-1 baseline), reducing peak perplexity by 64\\% ($56.99$ vs.\\ $157.09$; Figure~\\ref{fig:intervention}) in both Pythia-410M and 1.4B.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_intervention.pdf}\n\\caption{Canary-triggered intervention (Pythia-410M, $\\alpha{=}1.0$). Predictive cessation on entropy (generation~1) reduces peak perplexity by 64\\% versus reactive triggering on distinct-1 (generation~2).}\n\\label{fig:intervention}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Detection outcomes across contamination levels (Pythia-410M, seed~42, $T{=}10$, fp32). PermG: forward FDR-significant pairs; $K{\\geq}3$: consensus pairs (of 8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs).}\n\\label{tab:summary_detection}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$\\alpha$ & PermG & $K{\\geq}3$ & Regime \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.00 & 0/8 & 0/8 & null \\\\\n0.25 & 0/8 & 0/8 & blind \\\\\n0.45 & 0/8 & 0/8 & blind \\\\\n0.50 & 6/8 & 6/8 & onset \\\\\n0.60 & 0/8 & 0/8 & anomaly \\\\\n0.75 & 6/8 & 6/8 & detection \\\\\n1.00 & 6/8 & 6/8 & detection \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Consensus threshold operating characteristics ($K \\in \\{2,3,4,5\\}$). FPR: null-control rate (9 seeds, 54 pairs); TPR: detection rate at selected $\\alpha$ levels (Pythia-410M, $T{=}10$, fp32). $K{=}3$ balances FPR control (11.1\\%) against detection power (75\\% at $\\alpha{=}1.0$); $K{=}4$ eliminates false positives but reduces TPR to ${\\leq}25\\%$.}\n\\label{tab:mmct_k_operating}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& $K{\\geq}2$ & $K{\\geq}3$ & $K{\\geq}4$ & $K{=}5$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nFPR ($\\alpha{=}0.0$) & 38.9\\% & 11.1\\% & 0\\% & 0\\% \\\\\nTPR ($\\alpha{=}0.50$) & 85.4\\% & 66.7\\% & 25.0\\% & 4.2\\% \\\\\nTPR ($\\alpha{=}0.75$) & 72.9\\% & 37.5\\% & 0\\% & 0\\% \\\\\nTPR ($\\alpha{=}1.0$) & 75.0\\% & 75.0\\% & 25.0\\% & 0\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013yz15Q7coFoScWVGY93KJi`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Cascade Ordering}
5	\label{sec:cascade}
6	
7	If distributional collapse follows a predictable ordering across metrics, the earliest-responding metric can serve as a canary signal.
8	We formalize known metric properties---entropy as a continuous indicator of mode concentration, diversity as a threshold indicator of lexical coverage---under mode amplification~\citep{shumailov2024curse,dohmatob2024tale} (derivations in Appendix~\ref{app:cascade_theory}).
9	
10	\begin{designprinciple}[Entropy responds before diversity]\label{thm:entropy_leads}
11	Under mode amplification: \textup{(a)} entropy can decrease while the $\epsilon$-effective support $S_\epsilon(p) {=} |\{i: p_i > \epsilon\}|$ (hence distinct-$n$) is unchanged; \textup{(b)} if distinct-$n$ decreases, entropy has already decreased.
12	\end{designprinciple}
13	
14	The asymmetry arises because entropy responds to any mass redistribution (first-order), while diversity changes only when a token probability crosses the generation threshold~$\epsilon$; the margin $\gamma = \min_{i: p_i > \epsilon}(p_i - \epsilon)$ quantifies how much shift diversity absorbs silently.
15	
16	\begin{designprinciple}[Diversity lag $\geq 1$ generation]\label{prop:div_lag}
17	If the per-generation perturbation satisfies $\alpha\|\mathcal{T}(p_t) - p_t\|_\infty < \gamma(p_t, \epsilon)$ through entropy onset $t_H$, then diversity onset $t_D \geq t_H + 1$.
18	\end{designprinciple}
19	
20	ECE co-moves with entropy at zero lag ($\Delta\mathrm{ECE} \approx \Delta C > 0$ whenever $\Delta\bar{H} < 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:cascade_theory}).
21	The predicted cascade $\{H, \mathrm{ECE}\} \to \mathrm{Distinct}$-$n$ holds in 14/15 experiments at $\alpha \geq 0.30$: entropy and ECE onset at generation~1, distinct-$n$ at generation~2 ($t_H{=}t_{\mathrm{ECE}}{=}1$, $t_D{=}2$ uniformly; \S\ref{sec:regime}; Appendix~\ref{app:moment_cascade}).
22	The assumption requires monotonic entropy decline; SmolLM3-3B (Table~\ref{tab:generalization}) provides an empirical counter-example.
23	
24	\subsection{Design Considerations}
25	\label{sec:pitfalls}
26	
27	We use Granger causality~\citep{granger1969investigating} to test temporal precedence---whether past canary values improve prediction of downstream metrics---not mechanistic causation; bivariate Granger tests cannot exclude confounders~\citep{shojaie2022granger}.
28	Lead-lag analysis in iterative model collapse is fragile along three axes:
29	\emph{Method sensitivity}: five detection methods applied to identical data yield forward significance rates spanning 0\% to 92\%, with $\kappa{=}0.185$.
30	\emph{Spurious regression}: first-differencing eliminates canary$\to$perplexity associations ($F{\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$) while preserving canary$\to$diversity (24/24$\to$24/24)~\citep{granger1974spurious}.
31	\emph{Regime dependence}: the same Granger test shifts from 0/8 to 6/8 significant pairs within a 2-percentage-point interval.
32	
33	\subsection{Method Sensitivity Analysis}
34	\label{sec:mmct}
35	
36	We compare five lead-lag detection methods---naive cross-correlation (NaiveXCorr), differenced cross-correlation (DiffXCorr), threshold onset, permutation Granger causality (PermG), and Toda--Yamamoto~\citep{toda1995statistical}---in a multi-method comparison~\citep{simonsohn2020specification} (algorithm in Appendix~\ref{app:mmct}), calibrated against null-control ($\alpha{=}0.0$) data.
37	Pooled null-control FPR across nine seeds (54 pairs): PermG 7.4\%, DiffXCorr 14.8\%, Threshold 24.1\%, Toda--Yamamoto 33.3\%, NaiveXCorr 94.4\% (Appendix~\ref{app:fpr_independence}).
38	PermG (4/54; 95\% CI $[2.1\%, 17.9\%]$) achieves the lowest FPR, combining directional separation with consistent detection at $\alpha \geq 0.50$ (Shapley analysis: Appendix~\ref{app:shapley}).
39	Detection results use equal-weight majority voting ($K{\geq}3$ of 5 methods) with forward-direction filtering; $K{=}3$ yields 11.1\% FPR with 75\% true positive rate (TPR) at $\alpha{=}1.0$ (Table~\ref{tab:mmct_k_operating}).
40	
41	\paragraph{Permutation Granger causality.}
42	PermG tests whether past values of canary metric $X$ improve prediction of downstream metric $Y$ beyond $Y$'s own history~\citep{shojaie2022granger}, using 5{,}000 permutations~\citep{phipson2010permutation} on first-differenced series with Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) FDR correction at $q{=}0.05$ across 8 canary$\to$downstream pairs.
43	The permutation null controls Type~I error by construction; BH-FDR remains valid under positive regression dependence on a subset (PRDS)~\citep{benjamini2001control}.
44	Empirically, 4/54 null-control rejections across nine seeds confirm adequate size control (7/9 seeds yield 0~FP; Appendix~\ref{app:fpr_independence}).
45	
46	\begin{figure}[t]
47	\centering
48	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_method_comparison.pdf}
49	\caption{Method sensitivity: five methods yield dramatically different FDR-significant rates on identical data. PermG achieves 0\% null-control FPR with 75\% detection at $\alpha{\geq}0.50$; dashed line: 5\% nominal.}
50	\label{fig:method_comparison}
51	\end{figure}
52	
53	\subsection{Detection Boundary}
54	\label{sec:theory}
55	
56	The detection boundary is consistent with power-law scaling in observation length:
57	\begin{equation}
58	\alpha_c(T) = A \cdot T^{-\gamma},
59	\label{eq:alpha-c}
60	\end{equation}
61	Under an empirical sub-linear signal model $S(\alpha) = c \cdot \alpha^\beta$ ($\beta < 1$), the exponent satisfies $\gamma = 1/(2\beta)$:
62	\begin{equation}
63	\alpha_c(T) = A \cdot T^{-1/(2\beta)},
64	\label{eq:alpha-c-theory}
65	\end{equation}
66	where $A = (z^* \sigma / c)^{1/\beta}$ and $z^* = \Phi^{-1}(\pi^*)$ (justification in Appendix~\ref{app:alpha_c_proof}).
67	Cross-$T$ validation yields $\gamma \in [0.64, 2.08]$ (profile 95\% CI); the qualitative conclusion---detection improves with longer observation---is robust to the specific $\gamma$ value (cross-validation details and shape analysis in Appendix~\ref{app:alpha_c_proof}).
68	Dense $\alpha$ sampling favors a smooth sigmoid over a step function (Akaike information criterion difference $\Delta\mathrm{AIC}{=}-110$), but the fitted onset steepness ($k{=}15.7$) exceeds smooth-model predictions by $3$--$4{\times}$ (Appendix~\ref{app:power_sim}).
69	
```

PermG achieves 0\\% null-control FPR with 75\\% detection at $\\alpha{\\geq}0.50$; dashed line: 5\\% nominal.}\n\\label{fig:method_comparison}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Detection Boundary}\n\\label{sec:theory}\n\nThe detection boundary is consistent with power-law scaling in observation length:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\alpha_c(T) = A \\cdot T^{-\\gamma},\n\\label{eq:alpha-c}\n\\end{equation}\nUnder an empirical sub-linear signal model $S(\\alpha) = c \\cdot \\alpha^\\beta$ ($\\beta < 1$), the exponent satisfies $\\gamma = 1/(2\\beta)$:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\alpha_c(T) = A \\cdot T^{-1/(2\\beta)},\n\\label{eq:alpha-c-theory}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $A = (z^* \\sigma / c)^{1/\\beta}$ and $z^* = \\Phi^{-1}(\\pi^*)$ (justification in Appendix~\\ref{app:alpha_c_proof}).\nCross-$T$ validation yields $\\gamma \\in [0.64, 2.08]$ (profile 95\\% CI); the qualitative conclusion---detection improves with longer observation---is robust to the specific $\\gamma$ value (cross-validation details and shape analysis in Appendix~\\ref{app:alpha_c_proof}).\nDense $\\alpha$ sampling favors a smooth sigmoid over a step function (Akaike information criterion difference $\\Delta\\mathrm{AIC}{=}-110$), but the fitted onset steepness ($k{=}15.7$) exceeds smooth-model predictions by $3$--$4{\\times}$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:power_sim}).\n",

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
5	\label{sec:setup}
6	
7	We study iterative training collapse using Pythia-410M~\citep{biderman2023pythia} as the primary architecture.
8	Training data is drawn from WikiText-103~\citep{merity2017pointer} following the replace paradigm of~\citet{shumailov2024curse}: each generation fine-tunes on a mixture of $\alpha$ synthetic and $(1{-}\alpha)$ real data, generates 5{,}000 replacement samples, and fully replaces the synthetic pool (hyperparameters in Appendix~\ref{app:training_details}).
9	We run $T{=}10$ generations across 13 contamination levels ($\alpha \in \{0.00, \ldots, 1.00\}$) with multi-seed replication, yielding ${\sim}1{,}500$ statistical tests after BH-FDR correction~\citep{benjamini1995controlling}.
10	We track two \emph{canary metrics} (token entropy and ECE) and three \emph{downstream metrics}: distinct-1, distinct-2~\citep{li2016diversity}, and perplexity (distinct-3: Appendix~\ref{app:distinct3}).
11	PermG uses 5{,}000 permutations on first-differenced series~\citep{shojaie2022granger} with maximum lag~5.
12	
13	
14	\subsection{Sharp Detection Onset}
15	\label{sec:regime}
16	
17	Diversity collapse occurs at every contamination level: distinct-1 declines by $14.1\%$ even at $\alpha{=}0.0$ (fine-tuning drift) and by $40.3{\pm}0.3\%$ at $\alpha{=}0.25$.
18	Despite this ubiquitous degradation, Granger detectability is sharply regime-dependent.
19	PermG finds 0/8 canary$\to$downstream pairs FDR-significant from $\alpha{=}0.00$ through $\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps to 6/8 (Clopper--Pearson 95\% CI $[0.25, 0.92]$) at $\alpha{=}0.50$, uniformly across all 3~fp32 seeds.
20	
21	This transition is striking because cumulative synthetic fraction barely changes across the boundary ($C{=}99.75\%$ at $\alpha{=}0.45$ vs.\ $99.90\%$ at $\alpha{=}0.50$), yet detection jumps from null to reliable.
22	Sub-threshold $p$-values at $\alpha{=}0.45$ ($p_{\mathrm{fdr}} = 0.059$--$0.091$) confirm proximity to the boundary.
23	Sigmoid regression yields $\alpha_c = 0.483 \pm 0.006$ ($R^2 = 0.969$; Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}); cross-seed consistency (3/3 seeds: $0/8{\to}6/8$) and specification-invariance across 1{,}160 configurations (Appendix~\ref{app:spec_curve}) argue for a genuine transition (Fisher exact: $p = 3.45 \times 10^{-11}$).
24	
25	\paragraph{Canary scoping.}
26	First-differencing scopes the canary claim precisely.
27	Canary$\to$diversity pairs retain unanimous FDR significance after first-differencing ($24/24 \to 24/24$ across 4~seeds at $\alpha{=}1.0$), while canary$\to$perplexity collapses entirely ($F \approx 0$, $p = 0.99$)~\citep{granger1974spurious}.
28	At $\alpha{=}1.0$, all 24 canary$\to$diversity pairs reach FDR significance in the forward direction with 0/24 reverse; entropy onset at generation~1 precedes distinct-$n$ at generation~2 in 16/18 WikiText conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:moment_cascade}).
29	
30	\paragraph{Blind zone.}
31	At $\alpha{=}0.10$, distinct-1 has already dropped by $39.6\%$, yet token entropy is indistinguishable from the null.
32	Below $\alpha_c$ at $T{=}10$, diversity collapse proceeds without any detectable Granger-precedence signal.
33	
34	\begin{figure}[t]
35	\centering
36	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/metric_trajectory.pdf}
37	\caption{Metric trajectories at $\alpha{=}1.0$ (Pythia-410M, 4~seeds). Token entropy drops at generation~1; distinct-1 declines from generations~2--3, with a consistent one-generation canary lead (shaded: inter-seed range).}
38	\label{fig:metric_trajectory}
39	\end{figure}
40	
41	\begin{figure}[t]
42	\centering
43	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_cascade_onset.pdf}
44	\caption{Cascade onset timeline (Pythia-410M, WikiText, fp32). Entropy and ECE reach onset at generation~1; distinct-$n$ follows at generation~2. The ordering $t_H \leq t_D$ holds across all conditions.}
45	\label{fig:cascade_onset}
46	\end{figure}
47	
48	\begin{figure}[t]
49	\centering
50	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_spec_curve.pdf}
51	\caption{Specification curve (1{,}160 configurations). The blind$\to$onset jump at $\alpha_c{\approx}0.50$ is robust across methods, pairs, and seeds.}
52	\label{fig:spec_curve}
53	\end{figure}
54	
55	
56	\subsection{Dose-Response and Detection Regimes}
57	\label{sec:dose_response}
58	
59	The PermG dose-response profile (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}; Appendix~\ref{app:dose_response}) reveals three confirmed regimes---\emph{blind} ($\alpha \leq 0.45$, 0/8), \emph{onset} ($\alpha \approx 0.50$, 6/8), and \emph{detection} ($\alpha{\geq}0.75$, 6/8)---plus a non-monotonic anomaly at $\alpha \approx 0.60$ (0/8).
60	At this level, all direction-checked methods fail (PermG 0/8, threshold onset 0/8, Toda--Yamamoto 1/8), while bidirectional DiffXCorr maintains detection (6/8), suggesting compressed temporal lag disrupts directional separation.
61	The anomaly dissolves by $T{=}25$ ($5/8$), consistent with $\sqrt{T}$ power scaling (Appendix~\ref{app:t20_comparison}).
62	
63	\begin{figure}[t]
64	\centering
65	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dose_response.pdf}
66	\caption{Dose-response: PermG detection rate with sigmoid fit ($\alpha_c{=}0.483$; shaded 95\% CI). Regime bands mark blind, onset, sync-dip, and detection zones.}
67	\label{fig:dose_response}
68	\end{figure}
69	
70	
71	\subsection{Detection Boundary Left-Shift}
72	\label{sec:t20}
73	
74	Longer observation windows push the detection boundary leftward: $\alpha_c(5){\geq}1.0$, $\alpha_c(10){=}0.487$, consistent with power-law scaling (\S\ref{sec:theory}).
75	The $\alpha{=}0.45$ column validates this: undetectable at $T{\leq}10$ (0/8), it crosses into reliable detection at $T{=}15$ (6/8, dual-seed) and remains there at $T{=}20$ and $T{=}25$.
76	
77	\begin{figure}[t]
78	\centering
79	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_detection_surface.pdf}
80	\caption{Detection rate surface over $\alpha \times T$ (PermG forward, 3~seeds). Dashed curve: fitted boundary $\alpha_c(T)$. The blind zone ($\alpha \leq 0.45$) shrinks as $T$ increases.}
81	\label{fig:detection_surface}
82	\end{figure}
83	
84	
85	\subsection{Cross-Architecture Validation}
86	\label{sec:generalization}
87	
88	We evaluate PermG at $\alpha{=}1.0$ across 15 model-seed combinations spanning five families (345M--6.9B) and two domains (Table~\ref{tab:generalization}; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_arch_table}).
89	Forward detection reaches ${\geq}6/8$ in 11/15 conditions (Figure~\ref{fig:cross_arch_heatmap}).
90	The four non-confirming conditions each isolate a distinct failure mode: precision sensitivity (GPT-2 bf16: 0/8 vs.\ fp32: 6/8), bidirectional confounding (GPT-2 s43), seed dependence (Pythia-1.4B: s42 2/8, s43 6/8), and entropy decoupling (SmolLM3-3B: 3/8).
91	Standalone PermG claims require architecture-specific null calibration (GPT-2 null FPR: 50\%; Appendix~\ref{app:fpr_independence}).
92	
93	\begin{figure}[t]
94	\centering
95	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_cross_arch_heatmap.pdf}
96	\caption{Cross-architecture validation ($\alpha{=}1.0$): PermG forward detection (${\geq}6/8$) in 11/15 model-seed conditions spanning five families (345M--6.9B); faded bars: non-confirming conditions.}
97	\label{fig:cross_arch_heatmap}
98	\end{figure}
99	
100	
101	\subsection{Canary-Based Intervention (Proof of Concept)}
102	\label{sec:intervention}
103	
104	As a proof-of-concept under favorable conditions ($\alpha{=}1.0$), we implement predictive cessation: training switches to pure real data when token entropy drops ${>}15\%$ from generation~0 (robust band $[10\%, 22\%]$; Appendix~\ref{app:intervention_sensitivity}).
105	The predictive trigger fires at generation~1 (one generation before a reactive distinct-1 baseline), reducing peak perplexity by 64\% ($56.99$ vs.\ $157.09$; Figure~\ref{fig:intervention}) in both Pythia-410M and 1.4B.
106	
107	\begin{figure}[t]
108	\centering
109	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_intervention.pdf}
110	\caption{Canary-triggered intervention (Pythia-410M, $\alpha{=}1.0$). Predictive cessation on entropy (generation~1) reduces peak perplexity by 64\% versus reactive triggering on distinct-1 (generation~2).}
111	\label{fig:intervention}
112	\end{figure}
113

115	\centering
